# Villmarks

A small wilderness MUD. The valley is already there: a brook, a still pool, a slope, a rock overhang, dry wood and berries under that rock, a pot, dark berries, mushrooms, an open pile, one heavy log, one loose stone on the slope at (2,0), one flint nodule on the slope at (4,0), two lumps of clay on the bank at (2,2), and one deer on the trail. A bees' nest hangs in a tree on the east slope, at (6, 0). People and external agents use the same rules. Rain soaks whatever the sky can reach. Bark on a frame supported at both ends is a roof; a half-finished frame is not. The same bark, with a dry branch, is tinder for a fire.

The simulation is a Rust library with no network, database, clock, or model calls. One Tokio and Axum process owns the valley. A TypeScript page and a Python client are players, not special cases. Design choices taken from EmpireMUD, Bunnyland, Aardwolf, and GemStone III are in [DESIGN.md](DESIGN.md). Nothing here is deployed.

## Run it

Rust 1.99.1 is pinned in `rust-toolchain.toml`. You need Node and Python 3.

```bash
cargo build -p villmarks-server
cd web && npm install && npm run build && cd ..
cargo run -p villmarks-server
```

The valley listens on [http://127.0.0.1:18431](http://127.0.0.1:18431). The action window is 20 seconds (`config/evening.toml`). Open a second browser tab and take a different name. `sessionStorage` keeps each tab's token.

```bash
python3 agent/play.py --name Linde
```

That client uses a scripted policy and no model key. To let an external model pick from the same allowed actions, set `VILLMARKS_MODEL_URL`, `VILLMARKS_MODEL_NAME`, and `VILLMARKS_MODEL_KEY`. The world does not wait on the model.

Useful flags:

```bash
cargo run -p villmarks-server -- --help
cargo run -p villmarks-server -- --manual          # advance with POST /v1/advance
cargo run -p villmarks-server -- --window-secs 2   # shorter windows
cargo run -p villmarks-server -- --data data/valley.sqlite
```

Delete the sqlite file to reseed the valley. A living file keeps the tuning it was born with. The web dev server, if you want it, is `npm run dev` in `web/` on port 47321 and proxies the API.

Commands: `look`, `examine wood|bark|berries|dark berries|mushrooms|pot|mud|water|stone|flint|flake|clay|bowl|spear|carcass|hide|fire|deer|tracks|log|frame|rock|slope|self|here|<name>`, `say`, `n` `s` `e` `w`, `gather branch|bark|berries|dark berries|mushrooms|pot|mud|water|stone|flint|clay|bowl|spear|carcass|hide`, `drop`, `grab`, `drag n|s|e|w`, `release`, `raise frame`, `lay bark`, `drink`, `boil`, `set clay`, `bind spear`, `knap`, `haft`, `hunt`, `butcher`, `wear hide`, `light fire`, `take brand`, `cook`, `eat berries`, `eat mushrooms`, `eat meat`, `eat carcass`, `smear mud`, `interrupt`, `help`.

One loose stone lies on the slope at (2,0). `gather stone` lifts it. From then on it is one carried thing with a stable id, and it is no longer on the ground. `drop stone` puts that same stone back where you stand. Someone outside sight does not see it. It is spoken as one stone: "A stone lies in contact with the ground here, in the open. The stone is dry. The sky is open above it. It is a found stone." In the arms, examine is one sentence: "A dry stone is carried by you, in the open, and it is a found stone." The id stays on the server. Branches and berries stay quantities.

A carried sheet of bark is one sentence: "A dry sheet of bark is carried by you, under the rock overhang." A carried pot is one sentence: "A dry pot is carried by you, under the rock overhang, and it can boil water over a fire." Dry sits inside the sentence. Look says "a dry sheet of bark" and "a dry pot". In the arms, one sheet of bark stays "a dry sheet of bark", two are "two dry sheets of bark", and three are "three dry sheets of bark". One lump of ochre stays "a lump of ochre", and two are "two lumps of ochre". In the arms, one mushroom is "a dry mushroom" and two are "two dry mushrooms". A handful of berries is "a dry handful of berries". Examine of that carried food uses the same words: "Two dry mushrooms are carried by you, under the rock overhang." and "A dry handful of berries is carried by you, under the rock overhang. They are good to eat raw." Dry sits inside the sentence. One stone stays "a dry stone". One pot stays "A dry pot is carried by you, …, and it can boil water over a fire." One spear stays "a spear with an unhardened point" or "a spear with a hardened point". One pot on the ground is a pot. Look names a single thing on the ground, including a pot just dropped ("A pot sits here.") and one sheet of bark ("A sheet of bark lies here."). A count of one is not called a pile. Two or more stay a pile, and look names that pile too ("A pile of bark lies here.", "A pile of branches lies here."). The map still draws one letter. When the cell holds something that letter does not show, the next mark is `+`. The prose is the list. Piles of branches and berries stay quantities.

Two lumps of clay lie on the bank at (2,2), on the valley floor beside the brook and the still pool. `gather clay` lifts one lump. `set clay` while you stand at a burning fire puts that lump on the ground. Four ticks later (`clay_ticks` in `config/evening.toml`), if the fire is still burning, the clay hardens into a bowl. While it is hardening, look says "A lump of clay is hardening in the fire." on every tick of that wait. The sentence stops when the bowl is there. `gather bowl` lifts it. `boil` with the bowl works the same as with the pot: the water is drunk, and the bowl is not used up. Unfired clay cannot boil water. If the fire is out when those four ticks are up, the clay stays soft. Someone outside sight does not see the clay. There is no kiln, no wheel, and no metal. The seeded pot stays under the rock.

`bind spear` spends one carried stone and one carried branch and leaves one spear in the arms. The stone and the branch are gone, and neither is copied onto the ground. The spear keeps one id. Holding that spear on a burning fire hardens the point after four ticks (`spear_ticks` in `config/evening.toml`). While the point is hardening, look says "The point of your spear is hardening in the fire." on every tick of that wait. The sentence stops when the point has hardened. The same id is still the spear. A nearby character reads "A spear with an unhardened point is carried by …" before that, and "A spear with a hardened point is carried by …" after. Look in the arms says "a spear with an unhardened point" or "a spear with a hardened point". On the ground, look says "A spear lies here. The point is not hardened." or "A spear lies here. The point is hardened." A spear left on the ground, or carried away from the fire, does not harden. A spear on the ground is the letter `j`. A flint-tipped spear uses that same wait on the fire. While the tip is hardening, look says "The flint tip of your spear is hardening in the fire." When the four ticks are up, the person who held it reads "You harden the flint tip of the spear in the fire." Someone who can see it reads "Near hardens the flint tip of the spear in the fire." The arms line is then "You have a spear with a fire-hardened flint tip."

`hunt` needs a hardened spear, and the deer on your cell or the next one. An unhardened spear is refused and does not wound the deer. On the deer's own cell the point brings it down. On the next cell the conditions are even enough for a recorded draw: the deer is killed, missed, or bolts. The same seed repeats that draw. A miss or a bolt leaves the deer alive and moved. A kill leaves the deer gone and one carcass on that cell. `gather carcass` lifts it. `eat carcass` is refused: "The carcass must be cooked." The carcass stays in the arms, and the hunger does not ease. `cook` at a fire is what turns the carcass into food. The result says the hunger word the body already uses. When the energy word also changes, it says that too: rested, tiring, or spent. When the energy word stays, it does not add an energy line. Someone who cannot see the deer does not see the kill. The spear is not used up.

Thirst rises every tick, whether or not anyone is here. Rain wets clothes and does not quench it. `drink` works in the brook or in the still pool at (2,1). A raw drink eases thirst and rolls a recorded draw. Running water, the brook, turns your stomach only occasionally (two times in ten, in this valley). Still water does it almost every time (nine times in ten). The illness amount stays on the server. You hear "well" or "ill". It fades on its own, a little every tick, until you are well again. `gather water` at either source, carry it to a fire, and `boil` it. Boiling needs the pot that sits with the supplies under the rock, or a bowl fired from the clay. The pot is not used up, and neither is the bowl. Unfired clay cannot boil water. The boiled drink eases thirst and does not roll that draw. It also does not cure an illness you already have. Hunger rises the same way thirst does. Energy does too: "rested", "tiring", or "spent". Bark is tinder. `light fire` spends one dry sheet of bark and one dry branch from the arms when those are carried, and from the pile at the feet only when the arms do not already hold that fuel. The line says which one was used. Rain puts that fire out in the open; the rock overhang or a bark roof keeps it. Sweet berries, the pile under the rock, can be eaten raw and give energy. They do not ease hunger. The handfuls at (4,1) are poisonous. Eating them turns your stomach and does not feed you, and cooking them does not make them food. Mushrooms at (3,1) must be heat-treated; raw mushrooms are refused and stay where they were. `cook` works only while a fire is burning where you stand, and it takes a mushroom first, then sweet berries, from your arms or from the ground at your feet. Bark is still tinder, not food. One raw drink, one boiled drink, one raw handful of sweet berries, or one cooked portion takes the edge off. Those amounts stay on the server. You hear "not thirsty", "thirsty", or "parched", and "not hungry", "hungry", or "starving".

A deer is already on the trail at (2,3). It walks one cell every tick, including when nobody has sent a command, along the trail and then to the berries under the rock. If it is standing on a pile of berries it eats one handful and stays for that tick. The handful leaves the pile. It is not a new item. The earlier handfuls are "A deer eats a handful of berries." The last one is "A deer eats the last handful of berries." While the deer is on the pile and eating, look says "A deer is eating the berries here." It does not also say berries lie here and a deer stands here. When the pile is gone, look does not say berries still lie there. Someone who cannot see that cell does not get the line. While the deer is eating, `examine berries` says "A deer is eating them." One handful is "A handful of berries", not a pile. When the deer is not eating them, the deer is not added. Someone who cannot see that cell still hears "You see no berries from here." Someone standing where the deer ate the last handful, while that deer is still on the cell, hears "The berries that were here are gone. The deer here ate them." Once that deer has left, it is "The berries that were here are gone. A deer ate them." If the deer did not eat them, the sentence stays "The berries that were here are gone." Someone who cannot see that cell still hears "You see no berries from here." While that deer is still on the cell, `examine deer` adds "It ate the berries here." From the next cell it is "It ate the berries there." Once the deer has left, that sentence is not added. A deer that did not eat them is not described as having eaten. Someone who cannot see the deer cannot examine it. The pile is not put back. The cell it walks off keeps hoofprints, and those prints stay. `examine tracks` names each visible mark once, by how far it lies, so two marks that share a direction are not the same sentence twice. Marks out of sight stay out. `examine deer` places the deer the same way: how many cells south, north, east, or west. "To the south and west" is not enough when more than one cell lies that way. Someone who cannot see the deer cannot examine it. Look names a deer on any cell you can see, including a diagonal that is not due west or due south. When that deer is standing on the loose branches, look says it in one sentence, with the cell count: "A deer stands on the rotting wood one cell south and one cell west, in the open." It does not also say the wood is rotting there and, separately, that a deer stands there. `examine deer` uses that same sentence while the deer is on the wood. When the deer has stepped off, the wood leaves the deer sentence. When hoofprints are on that same wood, the look line that names it also says they are there: "Wood left in the open is rotting one cell south and one cell west, and hoofprints mark the ground there." When that wood has no hoofprints, the line does not add them. Someone who cannot see the cell does not get the line. When the deer is not on the wood, look keeps the two facts apart. Someone who cannot see that cell does not get the deer, and cannot examine it. `examine deer` and `examine tracks` are the same examine command as the wood and the fire. There is no separate animal command. A person sees the deer, the prints, and what it just did only inside their own sight. Someone who arrives later can still find the prints.

The wet season arrives at tick 18, while the rain is already falling, whether or not anyone has sent a command. From then on, rain in the open soaks wood twice as fast. The rock overhang and a bark roof keep the same shelter. Nothing dries. Someone who can see that open wood reads "The rain is heavier on the wood this season." Someone who cannot see it does not get that line, or the event on that ground. The commands stay the same.

Loose branches left in the open rot. Every 8 ticks one of them is gone, with or without a command. The line is "Wood left in the open is rotting one cell south and one cell west." from the person looking, the same cell count `examine wood` uses. "in the open" appears once. Someone who cannot see that wood does not get the line. When one goes, the event says where, in cells from the hearer, the same way tracks do: "A branch rots away one cell north and one cell east, in the open. You hear branches breaking." "You hear branches breaking." stays with that place. Someone who cannot hear it does not get the line. The pile stays where it was. `examine wood` uses that same cell count from the person looking. When hoofprints are on that wood, `examine wood` says so in that sentence: "A pile of 3 branches is in contact with the ground one cell south and one cell west, in the open, and hoofprints mark the ground there." When that wood has no hoofprints, they are not added. When a deer is standing on the wood you examine, `examine wood` says so. If it is eating berries there, the sentence says "A deer is eating the berries there." On that cell it is "A deer is eating the berries here." If it is only standing on the rotting branches, the sentence says "A deer stands on the rotting branches." On the tick the deer steps onto the branches and a branch snaps, before a handful is gone, the sentence says "A deer stands on these branches." It does not say the deer is eating. The berries are still 4 and the branches are 3. When a handful goes on that tick, it says the deer is eating and that a handful is gone: "A deer is eating the berries here. A handful is gone." The berries go from 4 to 3, and the branches stay 3. If that handful is the last, it says "The last handful is gone." On a tick when no handful goes, it does not say one is gone. On the tick the deer arrives and a branch snaps, look says "A deer stands on these branches." It does not say the deer is eating. The rock is still 4 berries and 3 branches. The first time a handful goes and more than one handful is still on the cell, look says "A deer is eating the berries here. A handful is gone. More are left." The berries go from 4 to 3, and the branches stay 3. When a later handful goes and more than one still remain, look says "A deer is eating the berries here. Another handful is gone. More are left." It does not repeat the first sentence. When exactly one handful remains, look says "A deer is eating the berries here. This handful is the last." It does not wait until the pile hits 0 to say that. If that handful is the last and the pile is empty, look does not say the deer is eating, and it does not also say "The last handful is gone." It says the berries once: "The deer standing here ate the berries that were here." It does not also say "The berries that were here are gone" before the pot. It does not say "The deer here ate them," "A deer stands here," or "The last handful is gone." Then the pot, the branches, and the bark. Once the deer has left, it does not say the deer stands here. While that deer is still in sight and no neighbor line already says a deer, look says it stands there, placed the same way: "The deer one cell west stands there." When the neighbor line already says a deer, look does not count the cells again and does not add a standing sentence. After that line, one sentence says "That deer ate the berries that were here." When that deer is out of sight, the standing sentence is dropped. While a handful remains and the deer is eating, look keeps "A deer is eating the berries here." After that, look on that cell says "The deer standing here ate the berries that were here." while the deer is still on the cell. It does not also say "The berries that were here are gone" before the pot. After the deer has stepped off but is still in sight, look names that deer the way the rest of the look already places it: "The berries that were here are gone. The deer one cell south and one cell west ate them." When the deer is on the open wood and still in sight, the standing line is the way to turn: "The deer stands to the south and west." It does not say "that rotting wood" or "there," and it does not repeat "one cell south and one cell west stands there." South says "The corner beyond, to the west, has the rotting wood and the deer." West says "The corner beyond, to the south, has the rotting wood and the deer." South says that once: "The corner beyond, to the west, has the rotting wood and the deer, in the open, and hoofprints mark the wood under the deer." West does not say the corner again. It says "That way, to the south, reaches the same wood." There is no third sentence that says "that corner" again. West may still say the valley floor has hoofprints. While the deer is still on the rock, and on the tick it is one cell west, the rot line says "Wood left in the open is rotting to the south and west." South says "The corner beyond, to the west, has that wood." West says "That way, to the south, reaches the same wood." On that west tick West already says a deer is there. Look does not say "The deer one cell west ate them" or "The deer one cell west stands there." After that line, one sentence says "That deer ate the berries that were here." After the deer is out of sight, those wood lines stay. Before the compass, once, look says "The deer left hoofprints on the ground here." It does not say "Hoofprints mark the ground here" beside the pot, the branches, and the bark. After the compass it says "Hoofprints on that wood mark the deer's walk south off the valley floor on the wood's west side, onto the south floor, still in sight. The deer went on from the south floor onto the valley floor on the south floor's west side, in the open, not the valley floor one step to your west. A deer trail passes on that valley floor, hoofprints mark that valley floor, and the deer stands on that valley floor, out of sight." The floor on the wood's west side is the one South said the deer has left. The floor on the south floor's west side comes before the floor one step to your west. "Onto" is the arrival. It does not say "walked south from the valley floor to the wood's west," "onto the valley floor to the south floor's west," "from that valley floor," "past the south floor," "open valley floor," "west of the south floor," "it" after the trail, "the far floor," "the west floor," "that ground," "the deer is not there," "the deer is there," or "the cell." At sim time 14, under the rock, the deer is standing on the south floor. South says "The corner beyond, to the west, has that wood. Past that wood, to its west, is the valley floor on the wood's west side. The deer has left it. Hoofprints mark that ground. South of the valley floor on the wood's west side is the south floor, still in sight. The deer stands on the south floor." South is followed by West. After West it says "The deer ate the berries that were here." South has already said the deer stands on the south floor, so "The deer" is that deer. It does not write "That deer," and it does not write "on the south floor" on the verb. It does not sit before the compass, it does not also say the berries are gone, and it does not add "under the rock overhang." It does not also say the deer walked to the south floor. The sentence after that is the prints. Hoofprints on that wood mark the deer's walk onto the south floor. It does not repeat the meal, and it does not walk the deer onto the south floor again. It does not count those cells, and it does not also say "The deer two cells south and two cells west stands there" or "A deer stands two cells south and two cells west, in the open." At sim time 15 South says "The corner beyond, to the west, has that wood. Past that wood, to its west, is the valley floor on the wood's west side. The deer has left it. Hoofprints mark that ground. South of the valley floor on the wood's west side is the south floor, still in sight. Hoofprints mark the south floor." The passage's "onto the south floor" points at that line, and the passage does not say "Hoofprints mark the south floor" again. At sim time 10, 11, and 12, South does not say "the south floor." It does not say "the cell the deer entered." West stays "valley floor, hoofprints," and that way, to the south, reaches the same wood. It does not count the corner, it does not say hoofprints mark the ground there, and it does not say the deer is still on the wood. From off the rock the cell count stays, and the line says the deer left hoofprints on the rotting wood. The place lines do not add a second bearing. They do not say "one cell south and one cell west," and they do not say "The deer stands to the south and west." After South has named that wood, look says "The deer on that wood ate the berries." It does not say "ate them" once the compass sits between that sentence and the word berries. That look does not add the counted rot line beside South. On the empty pile it still says "A deer stands here" and "The deer here ate them." One cell west still says the placed ate line. Someone who cannot see the rock keeps the cell count: "A deer stands on the rotting wood one cell north and two cells east, in the open, and hoofprints mark the rotting wood under the deer." They do not hear hoofprints mark the ground there for that wood. One cell west, where the west line shows the deer, it still says "The deer one cell west stands there." When that deer is no longer in sight, it says "The berries that were here are gone. A deer ate them." and does not say the deer stands. It does not say the deer is still here or still eating. The berries are not put back. On a tick when no handful goes, look does not say one is gone. Someone who cannot see that cell does not get the line. When no deer is on that wood, one is not added. Someone who cannot see the wood still cannot examine it. "To the north and east" is not enough when that names the neighboring diagonal instead of the pile. Someone who cannot see the wood cannot examine it. A rock overhang or a bark roof stops that, because the sky is no longer open on that cell. A branch built into a frame is not loose, so it is not in the rot. Bark, berries, mushrooms, and the log are not this rot. Someone who cannot see the open wood does not get the line, and does not hear the break.

When the deer steps onto a pile of loose branches, one branch from that pile snaps and the count goes down by one. The line says a branch snapped under the deer, and where, in cells from the hearer: "A deer steps east. A branch snapped under the deer one cell north and one cell east, in the open." A step that does not land on a pile does not say branches are breaking and does not change a pile. It does not snap more than one branch. Someone who cannot see that step does not get the line. Gathering a branch is "You gather a branch." for the gatherer and "Near gathers a branch." for someone who can see it. Hearing reaches one cell past sight. From there the line is "You hear branches breaking to the north and east," or whichever way the compass already names. Farther than that, nothing. A private miss stays with the person who missed. `build lean-to` (or `build shelter`) takes three dry branches and raises a lean-to in the cell. The line is "You build a lean-to of three branches." Someone who can see it reads "Near builds a lean-to of three branches." Look says "A lean-to of branches stands here. It breaks the wind." The body line changes from "You are in the wind." to "You are in a little wind." Standing there, the cold climbs slower than in the open. Without three dry branches, only the builder hears "You need three dry branches to build a lean-to." Rain does not wash a smear off someone standing under that lean-to. In the open it still does: "The rain washes the mud off you." or "The rain washes the red ochre off you." While rain is falling, look under the lean-to adds "Only a little rain reaches you under it, and it does not wash you." Someone who can see the lean-to from another cell reads "The rain does not wash anyone under it." Eating berries or lighting a fire stays quiet. Sight is still two cells.

A body in the open gets cold, and the wind is on them. The same exposure the rain already uses decides it: the rock overhang stops it, and the cold already on a body leaves once they stand under it, a bark roof lets a little through, open sky lets the wind double the cold. A body standing on a burning fire warms, and that tick does not also add the sky's cold. For a few ticks after they step off, or after the fire goes out, the sky's cold is halved, and the wind still cools them. While that leftover is what keeps the word warm, the look says so, and the sentence goes when the word leaves warm. The next cell does not warm. Someone standing there reads that the heat does not reach them. Rain still puts an open fire out. The sun on that body is unchanged. The sun burns bare skin while the rain is still off. `gather mud` at the brook or the still pool, then `smear mud`. A smear leaves a quarter of that sun. Mud flakes off, and rain in the open washes it off. The words are "warm", "chilly", or "cold"; "out of the wind", "in a little wind", or "in the wind"; "unburned", "pink", or "burned"; "bare skin", "thin mud", or "mud-smeared". `examine rock` describes the overhang when you can see it. Under it, the sentence is "Rock leans overhead. You are out of the wind. Rain does not reach you." From a cell that can see it, the same shelter is said of the rock. Someone who cannot see the overhang cannot examine it. `examine slope` describes the slope when you can see it: "The slope is open ground. It runs north from where you stand." Someone who cannot see that slope cannot examine it. From tick 12 the still pool is frozen unless a fire burns on it. The brook keeps running. The amounts stay on the server.

## An evening

Rain starts at tick 16. One person can still roof a frame before that if they take three branches and bark from under the rock and build on open ground. The open pile at (5,2) and the dry pile under the rock are the comparison. Two people can drag the log; one cannot.

This session was played through the player API against `villmarks-server --manual` (three clients, then a fourth who walked out of sight). Sera and Otto took the log. Linde built the roof. The interrupted drag did not move the log; the next one did.

```text
t7  The heavy log is in contact with the ground here, in the open. It is dry.
    Otto and Sera are gripping it.
t14 A frame stands here, under a bark roof. Supports: 2. It has a beam.
    It is supported at both ends. Bark lies over it and is dry.
    The wood in the frame is dry. Only a little rain reaches it.
```

After the rain, Linde examined the wood:

```text
A pile of 1 branch is in contact with the ground to the east, under the rock
overhang. The wood is dry. Rain does not reach it.
A pile of 4 branches is in contact with the ground to the south, in the rain.
The wood is soaked. Rain reaches it.
```

The frame's own wood was damp, under soaked bark: wet bark is a worse roof than dry bark, and it is still less than open sky. Nils, standing at the west edge, got `You see no wood from here.` His map had no wood glyph.

Sera's map after the rain:

```text
  0  1  2  3  4  5  6
0 ?? ?? ^  ^  ^  ^  ^
1 ?? ?? =  .  .  pH Rw
2 ?? ?? .  .  @L .W .
3 ?? ?? .  .  .  .  .
4 ?? ?? .  .  .  .  .
```

`H` is the bark roof, `Rw` is wood under the rock, `.W` is the soaked pile, `@L` is Sera on the log.

## Checks

Run from this workspace on 2 November 2026.

| Check | Result |
| --- | --- |
| The eat that leaves a handful does not call it the last (manual server, seed 1, port 24130) | Pass, 21 November 2026. At event time 9 the eat was "A deer eats from the pile and leaves a handful of berries." It was not "A deer eats the last handful of berries." Near and Quiet had it. Seen did not. Under the rock at sim time 14, look said "A handful of berries lies here. It is good to eat raw. A deer ate the rest." Examine said the same handful and "A deer ate the rest." Neither said the berries are gone. |
| The leftover handful does not say the berries are gone (manual server, seed 1, port 24129) | Pass, 21 November 2026. Under the rock at sim time 14, look said "A handful of berries lies here. It is good to eat raw. A deer ate the rest." Examine said the same handful and "A deer ate the rest." Neither said the berries are gone. Seen, off the rock, did not get the handful line. |

| Waterborne illness through the player API (manual server, seed 1, two clients) | Pass. One raw drink at the brook made Sera ill. The same command id did not drink again. Otto did not see her stomach turn. Boiling over the rock fire left her ill and did not sicken her again. Five empty advances later she was well. |

Examine did not advance the clock. The scripted policy returned `e`, which was already allowed. |

| Sound, still water, and food through the player API (manual server, seed 1, port 18509) | Pass, 3 November 2026. At tick 0, examine left the clock alone and told sweet berries, bitter berries, mushrooms, and the still pool apart. A later examine of the brook, also without moving the clock, calls it running water that sickens only occasionally. The client had no chance number and no draw. The first deer step was "A deer steps east." At tick 4, Sera heard "A deer steps east. You hear branches breaking." and Otto at (0,4) did not. At tick 9 she heard "A branch rots away in the open. You hear branches breaking." Otto did not. "Nils gathers a branch. You hear branches breaking." reached Sera only. At tick 25 Nils was tiring and hungry; `eat berries` left him rested and still hungry, and the handful was gone. Raw mushrooms and bitter berries were refused and the piles stayed at 2. Cooking a mushroom took hunger from hungry to not hungry. Boiling with no pot failed and kept the water. With the pot, the boiled drink eased thirst, did not turn a stomach, and left illness well. The still-pool drink turned Nils's stomach. Otto's brook drink left him well. A later look and examine stayed on tick 51. The scripted policy returned `eat berries`, then `cook`, then `gather mushrooms`, and never `eat mushrooms`. |

| Rain evening through the player API (`python3 agent/evening_session.py` against `--manual`) | Pass. Dry rock pile, soaked open pile, bark roof, distant slice, duplicate grab id. |
| Opposite HTTP receive order for the same drag (`python3 agent/order_check.py`, seed 7) | Pass. Both orders finished at (4,2). |
| One branch, and an interrupted drag | Pass in `sim/tests/evening.rs`. The evening session also interrupted a live drag before it landed. |
| Stop and start while two people hold the log (`python3 agent/restart_check.py`, SIGINT) | Pass. Grips, cell, rain, and a drag due at tick 18 came back. One more advance moved the log to (4,2). The same grab id was not applied again. |
| Release load test | Pass as a local experiment. See below. |
| Two browser tabs and `agent/play.py` | Pass, on `--window-secs 2`. See below. |

Load test, release binary, 20 windows, rain not firing. Network is not included. SQLite is included only in the `sim+sqlite` rows.

```text
spread-move  actors=1000 mode=sim         windows=20 mean_us=1078 max_us=1263 exceeded_20s_window=false
spread-move  actors=1000 mode=sim+sqlite  windows=20 mean_us=5165 max_us=29485 exceeded_20s_window=false
brook-say    actors=100  mode=sim         windows=20 mean_us=63 max_us=74 exceeded_20s_window=false
brook-say    actors=100  mode=sim+sqlite  windows=20 mean_us=217 max_us=937 exceeded_20s_window=false
```

No window took longer than 20 seconds. The slowest sqlite window in the spread was about 29 milliseconds. This does not say what a hosted game of Aardwolf or GemStone's size would do.

The browser check used Chrome against `http://127.0.0.1:18431` with `--window-secs 2`. Two tabs joined as Sera and Otto. Sera looked, examined the wood, walked north from (4,3) to (4,2), and spoke. Otto heard her, walked onto the log, and showed up on Sera's map as `p`. On a longer 2 second run the same page's examine showed the rock pile dry and the open pile soaked. `python3 agent/play.py --name Linde` used that timed server as well: it queued moves, gathered under the rock, and was still there when rain began. The map type is 18px at the root and about 21px for the grid, so it stays readable when a project pane draws the page smaller. A 390px-wide viewport stacks the map over the log and leaves the command field in the page instead of covering the prose.

## Still unverified

- A live model call. The client path is in `agent/policy.py`. No key was set, so that branch was not executed.
- A checkout on another machine. The commands above were run here after `cargo build` and `npm run build`.
- The default 20 second window was not the clock for these passes. The scripted evening used `--manual`. The browser and `play.py` used `--window-secs 2`. `config/evening.toml` still says 20, and nothing in the measured load test exceeded that.

## Follow-ups

At sim time 14 the prints mark the walk already taken, onto the south floor, still in sight, where the deer stands. At sim time 15 hoofprints on that wood mark the deer's walk south off the valley floor on the wood's west side, across the south floor, then onto the open valley floor. At sim time 13, 14, and 15, under the rock, West says hoofprints mark the outside floor, and the rotting wood is the next square south of that floor. A person on the outside floor is named there. At sim time 14 and 15, West names the person on the poison berries west of that floor. A person on that square at sim time 13 reads "You stand on the outside floor. Hoofprints mark the ground. The rock overhang is the next square east. The rotting wood is the next square south." East from there names the rock overhang and the person standing on the valley floor under it, and hoofprints mark that floor. South from there is the rotting wood. The rot line and the deer line do not count cells. From the outside floor the deer line names the west floor. One square west, at sim time 14 and 15, the place line stays the valley floor in the open and says the outside floor is the next square east. East from there names the outside floor, the rock overhang, and the person standing on the valley floor under that rock. From that square the person on the deer trail west of the south floor is named. From the trail, the person on the poison berries north of the west floor is named. The rock stays out of Far's East. South from there names the west floor, then the south floor past it. At sim time 14 the deer stands on the south floor in that line. At sim time 15 hoofprints mark the south floor, and the deer stands in the open on the deer trail west of it. The rot line says the wood is rotting south of the outside floor. That look does not count cells. From the trail where Far stands, East names the south floor once the deer has reached it, then the west floor north of that floor, the rotting wood east of the west floor, and the outside floor north of that wood. The deer stays in the south-floor sentence. That continuation stays out of East before the deer has reached the south floor. At sim time 11 the deer stands on the outside floor. At sim time 13 the deer stands on the west floor. Those looks do not count cells. South under the rock still ends with the deer standing on the west floor. At sim time 14 the prints sentence still walks south off the west floor onto the south floor. At sim time 15 the look still ends on the south floor turning west out of sight. Far's lines at sim time 10 and 15 stay, and Far's floor is not renamed. A body that has gone cold in the open is warm and out of the wind once they stand under the rock. The step onto that overhang is "You walk east, under the rock overhang, out of the wind." A walk east that does not enter it stays "You walk east." Under the rock the other person is named with the rock, not at the end of the place line. Once rain is falling, a body with no shelter reads "The rain wets your clothes." The rain event says the same for that person. Under the rock the clothes line is "The rain does not wet your clothes." The overhang and the rain stay one sentence, and a step onto it is out of the wind and the rain. A snap and a rot keep the weather of their own tick, and from Far the wood in those lines is east of the west floor. Once that pile is gone, West from the rock says the last branch is gone from the next square south, and hoofprints mark that ground. The place line names the branches still under the rock. When the pile goes from one branch to none, the event says the last branch rots away, and you hear a branch break. At sim time 16, while that one branch is still there, the opening says the last branch is rotting to the south. South says the last branch, and the deer left hoofprints on the ground there. The underfoot prints stay on the outside floor, once. That look does not say "under it," "that ground," "Hoofprints mark that branch," "Wood left in the open," or "the rotting wood." West says the last branch is the next square south of the outside floor. At the arrival, West says the last branch is gone from the next square south. Sim time 13, 14, and 15 still say the rotting wood. An earlier rot still says a branch rots away, and you hear branches breaking. The rot names that square and does not count cells. The ground names stay. A fire lit under the rock on the step that ends at sim time 5 is still burning at sim time 13 and at sim time 17, when the rain is falling and does not reach that ground. The mark stays `@*+`. The rain does not put it out. It still goes out later. The walk where nobody lights a fire is unchanged at sim time 16. When the rain arrives, a person who can see the fire hears "Rain reaches the valley and finds whatever was left in the open. The rain does not reach the fire." Someone who cannot see the fire does not get that sentence. The place line keeps "A small fire burns here. It warms you." and does not add the rain to it. A dry look does not mention rain. Gathering water while a pot is carried says the drink goes into the pot. Boiling it names that pot. The pot stays carried, and the person stays well. Empty hands stay a drink of water. While the drink is in the pot, the arms line is "You have a pot with a drink of water in it." After the boil it is a dry pot again. The boil still says the thirst eases when the body was already not thirsty. Gathering a pot is one sentence. The person who takes it reads "You gather a pot." A person who can see the act reads "Near gathers a pot." A person who cannot see it gets no sentence. That sentence still stops at the noun. A look from someone who can see the rock places the pot on that ground while it is there, and places Near on that cell. Once the pot is in the arms, the look does not leave it on the ground. It says that person is carrying the pot. Their own arms line stays "You have a dry pot." Gathering a pot names the ground it left. Dry and holding still show up only on the arms line and on the comma of the standing line. Lighting a fire is one sentence: "You light a fire with" the fuel for the person who lights it, and "Near lights a fire with" that same fuel for someone who can see it. Someone who cannot see the act gets no light sentence. A look from someone who can see that rock says "A small fire burns there. It warms anyone standing at it." only while a fire is burning. From a look that is not standing at the fire, the warmth names the person who is: "It warms Near." The person standing at the fire reads "It warms you." Those sentences name who is warmed and do not say the heat is felt. Near's fire sentence does not meet "You are warm." Boiling the drink in a pot is one sentence, filled for the person reading it. The person who boils it reads "You boil the drink in the pot over the fire and drink it." "The thirst eases." follows only when that thirst word changes. If they were already not thirsty, the reply ends on the drink. A person who can see the act reads "Near boils the drink in the pot over the fire and drinks it." and does not hear a thirst clause. A person who cannot see it gets no boil sentence. The act then says nothing about a thirst that was already satisfied. Gathering water is one sentence, filled for the person reading it. With a pot carried at the brook, that person reads "You gather a drink of water from the brook into the pot." Someone who can see the act reads "Near gathers a drink of water from the brook into the pot." Empty hands leave out the pot. Still water is not called the brook. Someone who cannot see the act gets no sentence. While that brook water is in the pot, the arms line is "You have a pot with a drink of raw water in it." The gather sentence does not say raw. After the boil the drink is gone and the pot is dry again. Raw lives only on that arms line. One flint nodule lies on the slope at (4,0). The person standing on it reads "A flint nodule lies here." Someone who can see that ground reads it in the compass. The person who gathers it reads "You gather a flint nodule." Someone who can see the act reads "Near gathers a flint nodule." Someone who cannot see it gets no sentence. The arms line is "You have a dry flint nodule." With that nodule carried, knap reads "You knap the flint nodule into a sharp flake." Someone who can see the act reads "Near knaps the flint nodule into a sharp flake." Someone who cannot see it gets no sentence. The arms line is then "You have a sharp flint flake." Look and examine say it is sharp enough to cut or haft later. With that flake and a carried dry branch, haft reads "You haft the flint flake and a dry branch into a spear." Someone who can see the act reads "Near hafts the flint flake and a dry branch into a spear." Someone who cannot see it gets no sentence. The arms line is then "You have a spear with a flint tip, not yet fire-hardened." Look and examine say the same. Holding that spear on the fire starts the same four-tick wait a stone point uses. There is no separate command. While it waits, look says "The flint tip of your spear is hardening in the fire." When the wait ends, the person who held it reads "You harden the flint tip of the spear in the fire." Someone who can see the act reads "Near hardens the flint tip of the spear in the fire." Someone who cannot see it gets no sentence. The arms line is then "You have a spear with a fire-hardened flint tip." `hunt` with that spear, standing on the deer's own cell, reads "You bring the deer down with the flint spear." Someone who can see the act reads "Near brings the deer down with the flint spear." Someone who cannot see it gets no sentence. `butcher`, with that flint spear still in the arms and the carcass on the same cell, reads "You butcher the carcass into raw meat." Someone who can see the act reads "Near butchers the carcass into raw meat." Someone who cannot see it gets no sentence. The carcass is gone. Look says "Raw meat lies here." The spear stays in the arms. `cook` on that cell, with a fire burning, reads "You cook the raw meat over the fire." Someone who can see the act reads "Near cooks the raw meat over the fire." Someone who cannot see it gets no sentence. Look then says "Cooked meat lies here." `eat meat` takes one cooked portion. Two portions of raw meat remain on the ground. A deer hide lies with them. `gather hide` puts it in the arms. `wear hide` puts it on. In the open wind, the same wait that reads "You are cold." without it reads "You are chilly." with it on. The wind is still named. A failed knap is heard only by the person who tried. Someone who can see them gets no knap sentence and no failure sentence. A successful knap is still heard by someone who can see the act. Two mushrooms lie on the valley floor at (3,1). A raw one is not food. `cook` over a fire leaves it cooked. `eat mushrooms` then eases hunger. The raw refusal is heard only by the person who tried. Poison berries on the valley floor at (4,1) stay poisonous. `cook` with only those berries says "Cooking does not make poison berries food." and is heard only by the person who tried. Eating them turns the stomach. Sweet berries stay good to eat raw. Mud from the still pool, smeared on the skin, reads "You smear mud on your skin." Someone who can see the act reads "Near smears mud on their skin." Someone who cannot see it gets no smear sentence. In the same open sun, before the rain, the bare body reads "Your skin is burned." and "Your skin is bare skin." The mudded body reads "Your skin is pink." and "Your skin is mud-smeared." When that rain reaches an open body, the mud comes off. That person reads "The rain washes the mud off you." Someone who can see them reads "The rain washes the mud off West." The body line is "Your skin is bare skin." Under the rock the same rain leaves "Your skin is mud-smeared." and says nothing about washing it off. A raw drink from the still pool, on the tick after arrival under seed 1, says "You drink the still water. The thirst eases. Your stomach turns." The body says "You are ill." The same tick in the brook says "You drink from the brook. The thirst eases." The body says "You are well." Someone who can see the drink hears it. The stomach line stays with the drinker. Boiling that brook water in the pot does not roll the illness and does not cure one already there. Fifteen empty ticks after that illness, the body says "You are well." The person reads "Your stomach settles." Nobody else hears it. From tick 12 the still pool is frozen. Look says "The still water is frozen." `drink` and `gather water` fail with that sentence, and only the person who tried hears it. The brook keeps running. Someone who can see that pool, and is not standing on it, reads that the still water that way is frozen. From where Far stands, that is "The still water to the north and west is frozen." Before that tick Far does not name ice. West, who cannot see the pool, does not name it either. `eat bark`, on the ground or in the arms, is heard only by the person who tried: "Bark is tinder, not food." The sheet stays. Lighting a fire still spends it. West, who can see the rock, hears the light and not the refusal. Far, who cannot, hears neither. The wet season arrives on the empty step that ends at sim time 19. The look gains "The rain is heavier on the wood this season." At sim time 18 that sentence is not there. Near, Far, and West each read it. The next tick keeps it and does not announce it again. On that same step the poison berries in the open are soaked. Near reads "The poison berries to the north are soaked." Far reads them to the north and east, and West to the north and west. At sim time 18 they are damp, and the look does not say soaked. The berries under the rock were dry, and the rain does not reach them. By the season the deer has eaten that pile. A fire on the frozen pool reads "The fire is keeping the still water from freezing." `drink` is possible again and is still the still-water draw. Under seed 1 that drink was "You drink the still water. The thirst eases. Your stomach turns." Far heard the drink and not the stomach. West heard neither. When that fire is burning, Far reads "The fire is keeping the still water to the north and west from freezing." and does not name ice. Before the fire, Far still reads "The still water to the north and west is frozen." Near keeps "The fire is keeping the still water from freezing." West, who cannot see the pool, names neither. A fire-hardened flint spear on the deer's own cell still brings it down. From the next cell the same spear is a recorded draw. Under seed 1, Near at (2,2) hunts the deer at (2,3) on the step that starts at sim time 28 and misses. Near reads "You miss the deer with the flint spear. It moves off." Far, who can see that cell, reads "Near misses the deer with the flint spear. It moves off." West gets no sentence. The deer leaves (2,3). The spear stays in the arms. The same place on the step that starts at sim time 64 is the bolt. Near reads "The deer bolts. Your flint spear does not land." Far reads "The deer bolts. Near's flint spear does not land." West gets no sentence. The miss and the strike on the deer's own cell stay. `remove hide` takes a worn deer hide off and returns it to the arms. At (6,2) the body is warm with the hide on, and still warm on the step it comes off. Near reads "You remove the deer hide. The wind reaches you again." West reads "Near removes the deer hide." and does not hear the wind. Far gets no sentence. Seven empty advances later the body is cold. A second remove stays with the person who tried: "You are not wearing a deer hide." One lump of ochre lies on the slope at (3,0). From (4,1) Near reads "A lump of ochre lies on the slope to the north and west." West, who cannot see that cell, does not. `gather ochre` on that slope is "You gather a lump of ochre." Far, at (3,1), reads "Near gathers a lump of ochre." West gets no sentence. The lump is in the arms. From (4,1) `examine ochre` is "A lump of ochre lies on the slope to the north and west." Far, at (3,1), examines "A lump of ochre lies on the slope to the north." On the cell the look stays "A lump of ochre lies here." Examine there is "A lump of ochre lies here, on the slope." West reads "You see no ochre from here." On the slope, `smear ochre` is "You smear red ochre on your skin." Far reads "Near smears red ochre on their skin." West gets no smear sentence. The body line is "Your skin is red-ochre-smeared." It does not also say bare skin. After the rain, the line is "Your skin is bare skin." A carried lump marks that slope with `mark`: "You mark the ground with red ochre." Far reads "Near marks the ground with red ochre." West gets no sentence. Near's look is "A red ochre mark is on the ground here." Far's look is "A red ochre mark is on the ground on the slope to the north." The compass still says "North: slope, a red ochre mark, 1 person." Open rain washes that ground mark: "The rain washes the red ochre mark off the ground on the slope." Near and Far hear those same words. West does not. The look no longer names it. Under the rock the mark stays, and there is no wash sentence. The lump is spent. It does not write words. Far's examine of Near says "Their skin is red-ochre-smeared." Open rain is "The rain washes the red ochre off you." Far reads "The rain washes the red ochre off Near." It is not smelted. One lump of copper ore lies on the brook at (1,2), beside the clay bank. From (3,3) Near reads "A lump of copper ore lies on the brook to the north and west." Far, at (3,2), reads "A lump of copper ore lies on the brook to the west." From the clay bank the look says "A lump of copper ore lies on the brook to the west." The compass still says "West: brook, a lump of copper ore." West, who cannot see that cell, does not. On the cell the look says "You are on the brook" and "A lump of copper ore lies here." `gather copper ore` is "You gather a lump of copper ore." Far reads "Near gathers a lump of copper ore." West gets no sentence. The lump is in the arms. It is not a tool yet. Carried to a burning fire, `smelt` sets it down. The person reads "You set a lump of copper ore by the fire. It leaves your arms. It is not metal yet." Someone who can see the act reads "Near sets a lump of copper ore by the fire. It leaves their arms." Someone who cannot see it gets no sentence. For four ticks, the same wait as clay, the look says "A lump of copper ore is smelting in the fire." Then anyone who can see that fire reads "The copper ore smelts into a bead." The look says "A copper bead lies here. It is metal from the fire." `gather bead` and `gather copper` are "You gather a copper bead." Someone who can see the act reads "Near gathers a copper bead." Someone who cannot see it gets no sentence. The arms line is "You have a copper bead, metal from the fire." `examine bead` is "A copper bead is carried by you, under the rock overhang. It is metal from the fire." `hammer`, or `forge`, at that fire is "You hammer the copper bead into a copper awl at the fire." Someone who can see the act reads "Near hammers the copper bead into a copper awl at the fire." Someone who cannot see it gets no sentence. The bead is gone. The arms line is "You have a copper awl." `examine awl` is "A copper awl is carried by you, under the rock overhang. It can pierce hide." Away from a fire, the same command with a carried stone is "You hammer the copper bead into a copper awl with a stone." The stone stays in the arms. `pierce hide`, with that awl and a carried or worn deer hide, is "You pierce the deer hide with the copper awl. It is ready to sew." Someone who can see the act reads "Near pierces the deer hide with the copper awl. It is ready to sew." Someone who cannot see it gets no sentence. The awl stays. A carried hide is "a pierced deer hide, ready to sew." Worn, the body line is "The pierced deer hide is on you. It is ready to sew." `examine hide` is "A pierced deer hide is carried by you, under the rock overhang. It is ready to sew." The arms line names it as one phrase, "a pierced deer hide (ready to sew)". `sew hide` is "You sew the pierced deer hide into a cloak." Someone who can see the act reads "Near sews the pierced deer hide into a cloak." Someone who cannot see it gets no sentence. The pierced state is gone. The arms line is "a sewn deer-hide cloak." `wear hide` is "You wear the sewn deer-hide cloak." The body line is "The sewn deer-hide cloak is on you." It keeps off the cold the way a worn deer hide does, the same halving, not a stronger shelter. In the open wind, after the same idle advances, the cloaked body is still "You are warm." when the bare body is "You are chilly." `rest`, or `sleep`, under the rock is "You rest under the rock. You are rested." Someone who can see the act reads "Near rests under the rock." Someone who cannot see it gets no sentence. The body line is "You are rested." and still "You are out of the wind." In the open wind the same command is heard only by the person who tried: "The wind will not let you rest." Under a lean-to the same rest succeeds: "You rest under the lean-to. You are rested." Someone who can see it reads "Near rests under the lean-to." The open wind without that shelter still refuses, and only the person who tried hears it. Rest under the rock stays "You rest under the rock. You are rested." The body stays "You are tiring." and "You are in the wind." A walk south off that rock is "You walk south, out from under the rock overhang, into the wind." Someone who can see the move reads "Near walks south, out from under the rock overhang, into the wind." Someone who cannot see it gets no sentence. The way back stays "You walk north, under the rock overhang, out of the wind." Once rain is falling, the way out is "You walk south, out from under the rock overhang, into the wind and the rain." and the way back is "You walk north, under the rock overhang, out of the wind and the rain." A walk that does not cross that edge stays "You walk east." One lump of iron ore lies on the slope at (5,0), apart from the ochre and the flint. From (4,1) the look says "A lump of iron ore lies on the slope to the north and east." Someone who cannot see that cell does not. On the cell the look says "A lump of iron ore lies here." `gather iron ore` is "You gather a lump of iron ore." Someone who can see the act reads "Near gathers a lump of iron ore." Someone who cannot see it gets no sentence. The arms line is "a lump of iron ore." A small fire that smelts copper refuses it, and only the person who tried hears "The fire is not hot enough for iron. Feed it more fuel." `feed` spends one more dry branch, from the arms or the pile at the feet, and reads "You feed a dry branch into the fire. It burns hotter." Someone who can see that reads "Near feeds a dry branch into the fire. It burns hotter." Then `smelt` reads "You set a lump of iron ore by the fire. It leaves your arms. It is not iron yet." Someone who can see the act reads "Near sets a lump of iron ore by the fire. It leaves their arms." After the same wait as copper, "The iron ore smelts into a bloom." The bloom lies as "A bloom of iron lies here. It is metal from a hotter fire." It is not forged. Before `feed`, the look is "A small fire burns here. It warms you." Someone who can see it reads "A small fire burns there." After `feed`, the look is "A fire burns hotter here. It warms you." Someone who can see it reads "A fire burns hotter there." Someone who cannot see the fire does not. `hammer bloom` at that fire is "You hammer the bloom of iron into an iron knife at the fire." Someone who can see it reads "Near hammers the bloom of iron into an iron knife at the fire." With a stone, and no hotter fire, it is "You hammer the bloom of iron into an iron knife with a stone." The bloom is gone. Examine says "It can cut." Off the fire, with no stone, only the striker hears "You need a hotter fire or a stone to hammer the bloom." With both a flint spear and an iron knife in the arms, `butcher` uses the knife: "You butcher the carcass with the iron knife into three portions of raw meat and a hide." Someone who can see it reads "Near butchers the carcass with the iron knife into three portions of raw meat and a hide." Neither tool leaves the arms. Three portions of raw meat and a hide lie there. A flint spear alone still butchers with the old sentence. A knife alone still uses the knife sentence. `cut meat` with the iron knife splits one whole portion, from the arms before the ground: "You cut the raw meat into two smaller pieces with the iron knife." A cooked portion is "You cut the cooked meat into two smaller pieces with the iron knife." Someone who can see it reads the same sentence with the cutter's name. The knife stays. A smaller cooked piece eases half as much hunger. Without the knife, only the cutter hears "You need an iron knife to cut the meat." A piece already cut is not cut again. With the same knife and a whole deer hide, `cut hide` or `cut strips` is "You cut the deer hide into strips with the iron knife." Someone who can see it reads "Near cuts the deer hide into strips with the iron knife." The knife stays. The hide is gone. Look says "Hide strips lie here. They are cordage." In the arms they are "hide strips." Examine says they are cordage. Without the knife, only that person hears "You need an iron knife to cut the hide." Without a hide, only that person hears "You have no deer hide to cut." From (4,3), two lumps of clay on the bank at (2,2) are "Clay lies on the brook bank to the north and west." From beside them, "Clay lies on the brook bank to the west." On the cell, look still says "Clay lies on the bank here." `gather clay` is "You gather a lump of clay." Someone who can see it reads "Near gathers a lump of clay." The lump is in the arms, and one lump stays on the bank. `shape clay` (or `shape pot`) is "You shape the clay into an unfired pot." Someone who can see it reads "Near shapes the clay into an unfired pot." A lump in the arms becomes "an unfired clay pot" there. A lump on the cell becomes "An unfired clay pot lies here. It is not hard yet." Examine says "An unfired clay pot is carried by you" and "It is not hard yet." Without clay, only the shaper hears "You have no clay to shape." The pot is not fired. `fire pot` (or `bake pot`) at a burning fire is "You set the unfired pot by the fire. It leaves your arms. It is not hard yet." Someone who can see it reads "Near sets the unfired pot by the fire. It leaves their arms." Four ticks later, while the fire still burns, "The clay pot hardens. It can hold water." Examine says "It can boil water over a fire." `boil` is "You boil the drink in the pot over the fire and drink it." The fire is not used up. Before it is set by the fire, look says "an unfired clay pot" and examine says "It is not hard yet." While it hardens, look and examine pot say "An unfired clay pot is hardening in the fire. It is not done yet." Someone who can see the fire reads that it is not done yet. Once it has hardened, those wait words are gone. Without a pot, only the person hears "You have no unfired pot to fire." Without a fire, only they hear "There is no fire burning here." From (4,1) the flint on the slope is also a sentence: "A flint nodule lies on the slope to the north." The compass still says "North: slope, a flint nodule." Someone who cannot see that cell does not. `examine flint` from (4,1) is "A flint nodule lies on the slope to the north. It is a sharp stone." On the cell it is "A flint nodule lies here, on the slope. It is a sharp stone." It does not say only "in the open." The look sentences stay. Examine of the nodule, on the ground or in the arms, keeps "It is a sharp stone." and adds "It can be knapped." After knap, examine of the flake stays "It is sharp enough to cut or haft later." and does not say it can be knapped.
